ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U388	Today's ninth item on the agenda is the first reading of draft law 420 amending the Law on study grants and student loans and related other laws initiated by the Government of the Republic. The rapporteur is Ms Liina Kersna, Minister for Education and Science. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U389	Honourable Riigikogu! The Government of the Republic has submitted to the Riigikogu a draft law amending study grants and student loans and related other laws. The main focus of this draft is on changes in doctoral studies, with which we wish to increase the attractiveness of doctoral studies, make it more efficient to complete the study and thus ensure the post-growth of researchers both in the academic world and in Estonian society as a whole. In addition to doctoral studies, there are also changes to the draft regarding student loans and needs-based study support. We prepared the doctoral reform in close cooperation with our good partners since the second half of 2019. Universities, research and development institutions, entrepreneurs, other ministries and students were involved in this multi-annual work from the outset. With the draft, we want to reform doctoral studies so that most doctoral students are guaranteed the position of a junior researcher in universities, in a regularly evaluable research and development institution, or have a contract of employment in a non-university institution in the field of their doctoral thesis. In other words, doctoral students would be employees of research work with their employer. The draft will redesign the working conditions and environment of doctoral students, which will make it possible to fix and pay more clearly the tasks related to doctoral studies and thus increase the effectiveness of doctoral studies. Changes in doctoral studies are essential. The number of doctoral graduates remains low and clearly insufficient to change the structure of the economy and meet the needs of society. Our goal was that in 2020 300 doctoral degrees would be obtained in Estonia during the academic year, but at best the number of PhD graduates exceeded just 250 in 2017, after which it has fallen again. This means that the effectiveness of doctoral studies is low and current graduates are not enough to ensure the post-growth of top specialists with a doctoral degree. This, in turn, hampers the further development of a knowledge-based society. This situation has been brought about by the overemployment of doctoral students in other work not related to their research, both at the university and outside the university. Other work is often carried out on a full-time basis, which does not allow sufficient commitment to research. The current doctoral allowance does not ensure sufficient maintenance to allow full-time commitment to the doctoral thesis. The reform of doctoral studies is an important part of the new R &amp; D, innovation and entrepreneurship development agenda. We must continue to increase the efficiency and attractiveness of doctoral studies, including by supporting doctoral students to take up the position of junior researcher, which gives them social guarantees and guarantees their income. We must also continue to support doctoral studies on knowledge transfer, as the need for top specialists is also outside the academic sector. It is important to change the rhetoric so far, and doctoral students should be treated in the future, not as students, but as starting researchers, whose main work is doctoral studies and doctoral preparation, and who are paid for their work. But doctoral studies are not the only area covered by this bill. Another important area is the planned changes in student loan conditions. The number of student borrowers has been falling steadily since 2009, which shows that the current system may no longer meet the needs of students. Student borrowing is unreasonable in today's situation and the requirement for two guarantors to apply for student loans is unduly strict. According to the bill before you, the maximum interest rate should be one guarantor and six months Euribor +3% when applying for student loans. To a limited extent, we also change the basis for receiving needs-based learning support. Taking into account the principle laid down in the Aliens Act, according to which, when a temporary residence permit is granted in Estonia, sufficient legal income is required to enable him or her to cope with his or her living in Estonia, the conditions are changed so that the foreign student staying in Estonia with a temporary residence permit granted for study will no longer be entitled to need-based education allowance, as we assume that he or she has confirmed that he or she will be able to cope financially. The draft proposal proposes the start of a new academic year, i.e. 2022/2023, with a gradual transition to a new concept of doctoral studies up to 2026. The University of Tartu has already sent you a proposal that this law should enter into force not on 1 September, but at the beginning of August. By the author of the draft law, I fully support this amendment in order to implement it from 1 August next year. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U390	There are also questions for the rapporteur. Heidy Purga, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U391	Thank you very much, Mr President-in-Office. Minister, thank you very much. I have a question about the student loan part of this bill. It is very, very positive that, according to this amendment to the law, the guarantors will no longer have to be two but one. ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U429	We will move on to agenda item 10: First reading of the draft Family Conciliation Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. Already in Question Time, there was a very interesting debate about this. Against this background, I would like to ask the Minister for Social Protection Signe Riisalo to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U430	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! It is a pleasure to stand before you with a bill that solves a rather old problem. It is also a pleasure that this bill has been prepared for a long time, already in the previous government, and in cooperation with the courts, the Ministry of Justice and, in fact, the conciliators themselves. What kind of problem does this bill solve? Solves this problem, faced with 4 000 people every year, who go to court to argue about matters of child custody, the arrangements for communicating with the child, or the maintenance issues. About one fifth of the cases that the Social Insurance Board entered the Child Welfare Department are precisely cases of disputed parents. What does that mean for a child? The separation of parents is a loss and trauma for a child. If parents do not come to a friendly agreement in the interests of the child, seeing such a dispute is the child's continuing trauma. Moreover, arguing parents focus not on raising a child but on arguing themselves. It takes time to argue. He takes money and time not only from his parents, neglecting children, but also from our judicial system and the child protection system. The purpose of this draft is to provide an out-of-court system in order for disputes to find a solution that is not enforced, because any solution to a court order is a compromise that neither party fully likes and may be even less liked by one of the parties. This, in turn, leads to a frequent situation where these court orders are not enforced and bailiffs have to intervene in everyday family life. We have also seen dramatic events through the media when children are tried to pass on from one parent to another with the help of bailiffs. An out-of-court solution would be such if the family could turn to the Social Insurance Board independently, also electronically, by submitting an application, or at an early stage in the course of legal proceedings when referring the court. What we want to achieve is a lower burden on the courts and a number of positive agreements to ensure the well-being of children. What are the disputes that can be resolved in family mediation? First of all, surely a child's communication arrangement that parents can agree with each other in the presence of a family mediator. Similarly, the size of the child's livelihood, as well as the termination or modification of custody rights, is limited to the court's direction. This amendment would enter into force on 1 July 2023. But today's family law really allows us to make changes in custody, even if there is consensus and agreement between parents, only by court. This is exhausting and unjustified for both parents who have reached an agreement and for the judicial system itself. When I said earlier that about 4 000 families turn to the courts every year with various disputes, there are, of course, more children involved in these disputes. We want to reach 3,000 families with our reconciliation service. 44 family mediators have been prepared and trained for this purpose, with whom the Social Insurance Board has also planned to conclude a contract. They're working full-time. Although we know that this need may prove to be greater in the long term, we also have confidence, because we know that in Estonia family mediators have been trained since the mid-90s and that we will be able to obtain additional resources from the service providers. The service provider is the Social Insurance Board. The Social Insurance Board is responsible for ensuring the provision of conciliation services. The Social Insurance Institution is also a conciliation body within the meaning of the Conciliation Act. He organises training for family mediators, maintains the quality of service and supervises the service. According to this law, family mediation is also subject to requirements: higher education, necessary personal characteristics for the conduct of family mediation procedures, and certainly also to the exclusion requirements set out in the Child Protection Act, i.e. the person has not committed the crimes mentioned in § 20 of the Child Protection Act, and he has completed the training of the conciliator of the Social Insurance Board, i.e. basic training and the children's rights module. If there is a question as to why conciliators with a certificate of competency must go further through this basic training and children's rights module, then as I said earlier, family mediators have been trained in Estonia since the early nineties and many of them have not provided the service for a long time or have not done so at all. Similarly, the content of the training and also the needs of society have changed somewhat. Special cases include family mediation. Special cases may include situations of close relationship violence, abuse or neglect, or where one or both parties to the conciliation have a mental disorder or mental disability. In this case, we have family mediators who have been trained and trained to deal with these special cases. In these specific cases, there is no obligation to negotiate a family agreement, and the family may opt out of it and do not have to participate in it. There are two ways to reach the service. You can contact the Social Insurance Board directly, you can do so online, but also by contacting directly or directing the court. If the family has previously been in the local government's local government's field of view, this is usually the case, then the child protection worker from the local government can also refer the person from the child protection worker to the Social Insurance Board. Let it be said here that, in the light of all such litigation, the opinion of a child protection worker is mostly required for the protection of children's interests in court proceedings, and this opinion can also be incorporated in this situation. One important criterion for the provision of conciliation services, but also for its success, is certainly that both parties agree to participate in the conciliation process and, in the specific cases already mentioned, an additional risk assessment by the Social Insurance Board so that the weaker party is not harmed or damaged. If the service is directed by a court, there is no need to submit a separate application. The total number of conciliation meetings is estimated to be five and may be more justified if necessary, but this is the current average practice. Children's hearing and children's involvement in conciliation procedures are mandatory, just as it is mandatory under the Civil Procedure Act. Every child has the right to express his or her opinion and must be heard when matters concerning his or her way of life are decided. It is possible to waive hearing the child, but there must be a good reason for this. This can be a good reason, either for the child's state of health, for the case of abuse, or for trying to avoid repeated hearing of the child, in situations where the child has already been heard by a child protection worker or the police, his or her opinion has been fixed and can be used in the conciliation process. The conciliation procedure may prove unsuccessful and in that case the dispute must still be resumed before the courts. We very much hope that there will be as little and more of these unsuccessful reconciliations as possible that are successful. This is in any case more reasonable in terms of organising children's lives than the ongoing litigation. If the conciliation procedure is successful, the parental agreement will be concluded. It's done with the conciliator. This is already confirmed by the Social Insurance Board in the course of the administrative procedure. The Social Insurance Board shall not approve agreements which are manifestly contrary to the interests of the child or which are not objectively enforceable. If the conciliation procedure is unsuccessful, the parents will be provided with proof and the litigation will continue. If successful, it will be possible to make the agreement directly enforceable and will then be used as a parental agreement, as an enforceable document within the meaning of the Code of Enforcement Procedure. If the family has reached the service through a court, the court shall close the proceedings on the basis of conciliation. The agreement approved by the Social Insurance Board replaces the previous agreements or the corresponding decisions on the dispute. Public family mediation is financed from the state budget. The budget for the following year is set at EUR 520000. In addition, the next year will be EUR 131000 from the European Economic Area and Norwegian support framework for preparatory activities, including both training and the development of an IT solution. I very much hope that, as a result of the successful completion and processing of the draft, it will be possible for families from 1 September next year to formally avoid access to justice and obtain these agreements with family mediators. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U493	Thank you, good Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! You have received a draft law amending the Environmental Monitoring Act prepared by the Ministry of the Environment. The amendments lay down the legal bases for the provision of paid services by the Environmental Agency at the level of the Act. The maximum and minimum rates for paid services per type of service shall also be laid down and the mandate for the Minister for the Environment to establish a more precise list of paid services shall be laid down. The draft concerns a total of six paid-up services. They are a special weather forecast, a special forecast model, a pay-per-view syncoptic information telephone, the production of a helm, analysis of meteorological and hydrological data and calculations of hydrological engineering solutions. Prices are calculated on the basis of accounting calculations, i.e. real costs are the basis for the price of services and profits from this service are not directly generated. Perhaps the State wants to cover the additional costs [which arise] by providing [services] to companies. The prices included in the draft include all components, including labour costs, economic costs and overheads. The draft also sets out limits. This is enough to cover these components mentioned above. National environmental monitoring is otherwise generally financed from the state budget, but historically there has also been a demand for services outside the environmental programme, such as the analysis of meteorological and hydrological data and special weather forecasting. Year-on-year demand for such specific services is also rising. Many private and public legal entities, or customers, need these services on the basis of their individual and specific needs. However, it really needs to be taken into account that these are narrow solutions and that there is no public interest in these services. The aim of the amendments is to reduce the workload of the Environmental Agency and the administrative burden on customers. Own revenues allow personalised services to be provided, as the individual customer's specific needs are not matched by public services. I would like to continue to emphasise that all such weather data will be publicly available to all parties, including public service companies, but if companies want something special, this bill will allow it. So, in summary, this is a clarification of the legal bases for the environmental agency's paid services. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U509	We are moving to agenda item 12, still at a speedy pace: First reading of draft law amending the Land Valuation Act, Land Tax Act and other laws initiated by the Government of the Republic. I ask for a presentation of the speech chair of the Riigikogu, Minister of State Administration Jaak Aabi. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U510	Honourable Chairman! Honourable members of the Riigikogu! The Government of the Republic has initiated a draft law on land valuation, the Land Tax Act and other laws that I can introduce to you here. First of all, it has to be said that it really has been done for a long time. Whether it's beautiful is yours to judge. The draft regulates the conduct of the regular land assessment and the implementation of the results. A regular land assessment is a mass land assessment, where a market-based value is found for all cadastral units. The values found are the basis for determining the fees for land tax, contracts for the use of state land and also for the toleration of technical networks. The last assessment was carried out in 2001 on a regular basis. The current tax price for land has been far below market value in 20 years. The law then included a provision that the evaluation should be carried out on a regular basis, but was not determined by what regularity, although everyone understood that it should be assessed every four years. It's been 20 years. Since the tax price has changed so much from the market value in that period, it leaves people unaware of the real value of their land and can also cause damage to them when dealing with the land. Similarly, land tax and land use charges are no longer market-based, although they should be. It is estimated that the price of land has increased seven times on average over 20 years. The changes have been quite different from region to region and due to land use. Thus, the regular valuation of the land also leads to an increase in the tax price of the land and may be reflected in a higher land tax and higher usage charges for the land owner. The current package of legislative changes also has safeguards to mitigate the effects and the sharp rise in tax. First of all, I will introduce the proposed changes to the regular land valuation. The assessment will take place on a regular basis every fourth year. The law now also sets out the range when it must take place. The Land Board conducts mass assessments on the basis of international standards and national databases. Each plot receives an individual approximate market value, i.e. the tax price of the land. We have sufficient data on all real estate transactions and parcels of sufficient quality. Therefore, no incorrect land price zones will be used for this assessment as compared to 2001. The evaluation will be carried out in the second half of 2022. The results of the evaluation will be implemented according to the draft at the beginning of 2024. 2023 is an intermediate year, allowing landowners to get acquainted with the results and municipalities to deal thoroughly with new tax rates. Municipalities must set these rates six months before the new tax rates are enforced, i.e. no later than 1 July 2023. It should be borne in mind that, in the regular assessment of the land, the social value is wider than the [clearness] of the tax base of the land alone. Information about the market-based value of the land will be available free of charge to all from the land registry. If there is a need to decide on the price of the transaction, the tax price provides primary information about the price and reduces the risk of making an uninformed transaction. In addition, it allows smaller land-related transactions to be carried out with less money and time without having to order expert advice. Information about the value of the land will be available to everyone free of charge in the land registry. There is no doubt that the land tax will have an impact on the land tax when it is assessed regularly. Land tax receipts have remained at almost the same level since 2012. As the economy and wages have grown rapidly in the meantime, land tax burden has decreased over time. As you all know, Estonia's share of property taxes in GDP or total tax receipts is competitively lowest among OECD countries. All land tax is received by municipalities and forms part of the income base. It is therefore the duty of the State to regularly assess countries in order to ensure that the tax base is up-to-date and fair. In order to prevent land tax from becoming too large in the future, the draft provides for lower maximum tax rates. While the maximum rates currently range from 2 to 2.5% of the land tax price, they will be 0.5 to 1% in the future. This will significantly reduce the impact of the regular valuation on the rise in land tax. In the future, the municipality will be able to set three tax rates, separate rates for residential, commercial and commercial land. As I said, specific tax rates depend directly on decisions taken by local governments. The maximum rate is foreseen and within this limit the municipality can decide the rate itself. It is also possible for municipalities to set new rates in such a way that for most landowners, land tax does not rise at all and some may even fall. The value of some of the plots has risen significantly above the average and the owners of such land may be hit by a surge in tax. In order to prevent this, the draft provides for a limitation that land tax may not increase by more than 10% in any parcel per year compared to the previous year. The draft provides that this limit on annual growth will continue to apply to all subsequent evaluations and increases in tax rates. Land tax is an important source of income for municipalities. Following the tax exemption imposed in 2012, land tax rates have remained close to the current maximum permitted rates and tax receipts have not been able to increase. This has worsened the tax autonomy of municipalities, which we also have one of the smallest among OECD countries. If the municipality can freely decide on the rates according to the local people's agreement, it is, for example, the decision whether to apply a higher or lower country tax, then it can be decided accordingly whether to provide more or less public services. Of the sums due from the land tax, municipalities guarantee public goods, including the maintenance of roads and streets, which is particularly important for landowners. I believe that, following a regular assessment, municipalities are responsible for setting rates. The owners do not have to worry about the large rise in the land tax, as the tax exemption for the land under home is maintained according to the draft as it was introduced in 2012. In addition, an amendment to the law extends the exemption for the land under the law in such a way that, if, for example, commercial premises are located on the first floor of an apartment building, home owners would no longer have to pay land tax for the part of commercial land as from 2024. All you have to do is pay for housing. This change affects about 9700 homes. The cost of land taxation is also linked to compensation for the tolerability of utility networks and many charges for contracts for the use of state land. On average, the evaluation will raise farm fees four times, which may increase the interest of landowners in applying for farm benefits in the future. In addition, contracts for the use of State land relating to the price of land would become more market-conform. If the tax prices of the land correspond to the market value, they can also be used more widely in drawing up contracts. This would make it possible to save money and time by reducing the need for peer reviews. In order to prevent a sharp rise in user charges, the law sets a single 3% rate for land tax charges instead of the current fragmented system, where charges are 2 to 7% of the land tax price. Finally, I would like to thank all the officials from the Ministry of Finance, the Ministry of the Environment and the Land Administration who helped with the draft. The preparatory work has been carried out for almost two years; it has been very thorough. Involvement has also been very thorough when we talk about various interest groups. Regular land evaluation is expected for 20 years, and it makes sense to finally do so. As I said, it is also a duty of the state. All impacts have been thoroughly analysed in the draft and adequate solutions have been proposed. I ask the Riigikogu to support this bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U567	We will go to agenda item 13: Draft Law amending the State Assets Act initiated by the Government of the Republic 421. Minister of Finance Keit Pentus-Rosimannus gives an overview of this. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U568	Thank you, Mr President. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! The Government of the Republic has submitted to you a draft law amending the State Assets Act, which aims in particular at supplementing the principles of state participation in companies. This is a rather logical continuation of the debate on the basis of the principles of the Green Paper, the White Paper and the participatory policy of the State's participatory policy, which has already been approved by the government. The amendments and additions to the State Assets Act with this draft can be divided into six blocks: strategic governance of state holdings; principles of consideration for participation in state companies; roles and governance principles in the management of state holdings; ensuring the sustainability, accountability and transparency of companies; reporting mechanism for state participation; consolidation of the accounting services of state foundations. First of all, the first important addition to the strategic governance of state participations is the obligation imposed on the government to renew at least once every five years the principles of state participation policies in order to respond to changes in the area of justice and management practices. A well-thought-out and regularly updated participatory policy in this form will certainly support the state's participation in companies and will help to achieve this as intended, and will also guide the legislative design of the governance of holdings. Another important addition is the imposition of the owner's expectations on each company with a majority shareholding in the State and the obligation to renew them at least once every three years. This is already quite a well-functioning practice in itself and, by establishing it through law, systematically helps boards and boards to focus companies' activities and certainly to manage them more effectively by setting strategic sectoral and financial targets. Secondly, we are changing the principles of consideration for the participation of the State in companies. The fact that the general direction and objective of the State is to participate only in companies where it is necessary for reasons of public interest or for strategic consideration shall also be fixed. Earning income through participation in entrepreneurship is not the main objective of the state; it can only be short-term or an additional objective. The timeliness of the objectives, their achievement, and also whether the country continues to be justified in participating, need to be assessed on a regular basis. Such assessment should be carried out every three years in the future. Thirdly, we will continue to look and not only look, but we will also continue to find places where we can simplify the role and governance principles of state ownership. To this end, the draft will provide an opportunity to represent the country at the general meeting of the listed company at the chancellor of the Ministry or the deputy chancellor of the Ministry. In the case of a publicly listed company which has its own nomination committee, where the nomination committee set up under the State Assets Act is also represented, the representative of the State is no longer required to ask for a duplicate position from the so-called "nomination committee' of the State Assets Act for a vote. In order to reduce formal decisions, we give the holder the right to elect, on the basis of a proposal from the nomination committee, the Chairman of the Supervisory Board, together with the members of the Supervisory Board, immediately. By means of the draft, we will reduce the volume of data to be provided in the Council candidates' forms and specify the rights of the members of the Supervisory Board to the members of the Management Board when determining the performance fee and the non-compete compensation. Fourthly, the draft also contributes to ensuring that companies operate in a sustainable, responsible and transparent manner. The fact that the State must set an example in its activities and that it must also be public undertakings, through responsible organisation of its activities and relations with stakeholders, and by minimising the negative economic, social and environmental impact of its activities must be natural and self-evident. The reporting mechanism for public participation will be simplified by this draft. In the future, the owners of the holdings shall assess the performance of the companies in the decisions of the general meeting approving the annual accounts and on their basis, the Ministry of Finance shall prepare a consolidated report. It is presented as information now to the Government of the Republic, then to the Riigikogu and also to the National Audit Office. However, in order to reduce the administrative burden, it is proposed to refrain from providing the annual reports of the companies and the reports of the Council's activities to ministries, as the same information must be published by the associations on their websites. Today, the ability to use the Internet is likely to be so advanced that they can be found on these websites. Consolidation of national foundations' accounting services is also part of this draft. This will continue the implementation of the National Reform Action Plan, which provides for consolidation of accounting services to the National Support Services Centre. In conclusion, the amendments made to this draft are still all reflected in the desire to improve the quality of governance of public undertakings and foundations and at the same time reduce the administrative burden involved. This is indeed a long-prepared change. It is based on good international practice, as well as on recommendations from private and public experts. Thank you very much. I would certainly ask for this necessary draft to be supported.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U581	The 14th item is the draft law amending the Pensions Act, the Tax Administration Act and the Income Tax Act, initiated by the Government of the Republic, 468. Minister of Finance Keit Pentus-Rosimannus gives an overview of this. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U582	Thank you, Mr President. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I'd like to introduce you to a short bill on retirement pensions. The third pension pillar, which this bill primarily relates to, is more popular in Estonia than ever before, must be said. More than 167000 people have joined it, some 10,000 of them in the last year. So there is every reason to engage in the continuous development of the third pension pillar. The draft submitted to you for discussion will allow, if Parliament approves it, simply and conveniently, starting from the new year, to withdraw money in instalments from the third pillar fund, i.e. to receive a fixed-term pension from the third pillar. In practice, such a possibility to receive a pension from the third pillar has so far been lacking. The money collected by the third pillar pension fund may be transferred to the insurance undertaking to obtain the pension, i.e. to enter into a supplementary funded pension contract for that money. To date, a new application for each payment has to be submitted for direct withdrawal from the pension fund, which means that most of the money has been withdrawn at the same time. Thus, the draft adds a fund pension already known from the second pillar, which is a fixed-term pension paid by the Pension Centre, to the third pillar payout methods by taking money from all the third pillar pension funds where the pensioner invests his or her money. If the details of such a pension plan have already been agreed upon, then in the future everything will be done automatically for the person, i.e. the Pension Centre will also make the entire tax calculation. As with the second pillar fund pension, a person in the third pillar can always stop payments or withdraw money at the same time. The period of retirement is chosen by the person himself. If the time limit is determined on the basis of the average of the years of residence or is longer than that, the payments shall also be exempt from tax. In the case of a shorter period, payments are subject to a 10% tax. The corresponding amendments to the Income Tax Act have already entered into force on 1 January, but the tax exemption has so far been available in practice only for people who have withdrawn money from the third pillar on the basis of an insurance contract. There are also a few amendments to this draft concerning the second pillar. The first of these concerns the payment of the second pillar payments in a situation where the employer has incurred tax debts. The Tax Act amends the procedures for satisfying claims so that, at the first stage, payments of the second pillar of employees are always paid from it when the money is received. The second pillar payments are also not allowed to be deferred. The second amendment, which also relates to the second pillar, concerns the exemption from the tax on investment income, where there is currently no reference to investments made through the second pillar pension investment account. If the investment proceeds from the pension to the investment account, it should not be taxed at the moment, as the tax will have to be paid when the payment from the second pillar takes place. This correction will also be made to the Income Tax Act. Our proposal is that this draft should enter into force on 1 January 2022, i.e. 1 January next year, and amendments to the Tax Administration Act on 1 July next year, i.e. 1 July 2022. Such changes are in front of you. Please support them. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U583	Good minister! You don't seem to have any questions. Thank you! Now Vice-President of the Monetary Commission, Aivar Kokk. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U584	Good manager! Ladies and gentlemen, Dear Minister, The Finance Committee discussed the preparation for the first reading of the Savings Pensions Act initiated by the Government of the Republic, the Tax Administration Act and the Income Tax Act Amendment Act 468 including 18 October. The draft was presented by the Minister of Finance Keit Pentus-Rosimannus and attended by Lemmi Oro, Head of the Tax Policy Department of the Ministry of Finance, and Anneli Valgma, Adviser to the Insurance Policy Department, Kertu Fedotov. The purpose of the draft is to create a more convenient way for people who raise money in third pillar pension funds to withdraw money from funds as a pension when they reach retirement age. The draft establishes the conditions for the third pillar funds to also benefit from the method of payment of the fund pension familiar to the second pillar. The Minister gave an overview of the draft and pointed out that the draft would make it easier to withdraw the funds accumulated in the pension funds of the third pillar once the pension age reached the fund as a pension. The current system is that if you want to receive a third pillar pension in instalments or on a fixed-term basis, you must submit an application each time. It is bureaucratic and it is more in favour of withdrawing money at once. From now on, the person can choose for himself or herself the period during which he or she will be paid out of the third pillar pension fund. Payments shall be exempt if the period is determined on the basis of the average of the years of residence or is longer than that. If the time limit is set shorter, payments shall be subject to a 10% income tax. In principle, the possibility of withdrawing money from the third pillar becomes the same as in the second pillar. The amendments to the draft relating to the second pillar relate to the payment of payments in a situation where the employer has incurred tax debts. The Commission took the following consensual decisions: to propose a draft to be put on the plenary agenda by 27 October, to appoint a previous speaker to represent the lead committee, to propose that the first reading be closed and to propose that the deadline for amendments be set at 5.15 p.m. on 10 November. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U620	The 16th item is the draft law amending the competition law initiated by the Government of the Republic and related amendments to other laws, which is number 442 SE. First reading is underway, and Maris Lauri, or the Minister of Justice, will introduce it by the government. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U621	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! The aim of the draft amendment to the Competition Act and related amendments to other laws is to change the current model of the Competition Authority financing. Before explaining a specific change, I will take a brief look at the activities of the Competition Authority, as this will allow for a better understanding of the need for change. The tasks of the Competition Authority can currently in principle be divided into two. Firstly, competition market surveillance includes an assessment of the admissibility of unlawful agreements, the misuse of market power and the control of concentrations, i.e. concentrations. In addition, market analyses are carried out to assess the application of free competition in different sectors. In its role as an economic regulator, the Competition Authority deals with the price regulation of monopoly companies, regulates and organises the functioning of markets, carries out market surveillance, issues licences. In this role, the Competition Authority operates in the following areas: electricity, natural gas, district heating, public water supply and sewerage, mail, rail, ports and airports. In recent years, the Competition Authority has been added and in the near future a number of new, additional tasks have been added. First of all, one major change is coming with the transposition of the ECN+ Directive, which strengthens the powers of the Competition Authority, including in particular in the handling of prohibited agreements and also in litigation. This draft is under development and will soon go to the coordination circle. Compliance with commercial practices prohibited in food supply chains is a new supervisory sector, leading to an increase in the volume of work. And, of course, we need to hire new people to analyse the market and then monitor it. The amendments to the Electricity Market Act result from a number of and increasing tasks of the economic regulator, which are very much linked to the introduction of renewable energy and, more generally, to the development of this market. In the area of regulation, additional tasks have also come from synchronisation of electricity networks, as well as from rail and port law, for example. If we look at the processes that have taken place, there has been an increase in the number of concentrations in the field of competition, as well as more and more litigation and, of course, the latter need thorough preparation, because the other party to the competition disputes is generally well funded and also manned. The Estonian Competition Authority is small, with 45 employees and a budget of less than 2 million euros. In Latvia, for example, there are 170 employees, 245 in Lithuania, and a budget of 6.7 million and 9.1 million respectively. I would like to point out that the volume of the competition authority's work does not depend at all on population numbers, as there is usually one undertaking operating in monopoly markets, regardless of whether the population is 1.3, 1.6, 2 or 5 million. The Estonian Competition Authority has been very modestly funded in the European context. If we want better competition supervision and a stronger market regulator, the Competition Authority must be better funded and manned. So far, the Estonian Competition Authority has been funded from the state budget. Four other countries from 24 European countries use the same method of funding: Norway, France, Germany and Slovakia. Three of them are considerably richer than Estonia. Twelve countries finance competition monitoring through supervisory fees and seven countries use a mixed scheme. Now for the new funding scheme. According to the draft, Estonia will also switch to the mixed system. This means that regulatory tasks will be financed from supervisory fees in the future, but the control of concentrations and prohibited agreements in the context of market surveillance competition will continue to be financed from the state budget. Supervisory fees are imposed on monopoly companies, i.e. electricity and gas networks, district heating networks, public water supply and sewerage companies, universal postal service providers, ports and airports. The amount of the fee depends on the administrative burden. In those companies where price coordination takes place proactively, the remuneration is 0,2% of the revenue from sales. This would apply to district heating, electricity, natural gas, water and postal companies. And I would like to point out that this would not really apply to the monopoly markets, for example in the electricity market, to electricity producers, but to electricity networks. In the case of ex post price follow-up, the supervisory fee would be 0,02%, which would apply to airports and ports. These rates are thus established by law, which ensures that this rate is controlled and prevents possible undertakings from being affected by supervisory charges and also excludes inefficiencies that may result from excessive revenues. As regards the possible increase in administrative burdens, this increase remains modest. This is because the supervisory fee is determined on the basis of the same data as those provided in the price applications. The data must be submitted once a year, by 15 July, following the submission of the annual report. Payment is then made on the basis of the invoice submitted, i.e. once a year. The invoice is issued by 30 August and there is one month to pay, i.e. it will be due by the end of September. It must be acknowledged that technical solutions can certainly be improved, as well as data collection, i.e. more modern IT solutions can be created. This, of course, requires additional funding. However, with current financial resources, such a fundamental change is not possible. The supervisory fee may be added to the price of the service. So there will be no cost for regulated companies, but it will be for consumers. But the cost is still modest. According to the analysis, for example, the cost of water and sewerage, electricity and district heating for a family of three people living in a three-room apartment would increase by a total of 14.2 to 33.3 cents per month, or 0.17 to 0.26%. The costing, of course, depends on a particular service, for example, the price of water would increase with a supervisory fee of 0.2%, i.e. if the cost of a cubic metre was 1.5 euros, the price would increase by 0.3 cents. So a single price increase for water would be achieved if more than 3 cubic metres of water was consumed, which could be about one person per month of consumption. The electricity bill would increase by 0.09%, due to the increase in network charges. The other components of the electric bill wouldn't grow. It is estimated that it could be 8 cents a month on average. Gas consumers have 4.3% of the population in Estonia, the figures would increase by 0.09%, or 3 cents, according to previous year's figures. For other services, the increase would be even lower. What would change as a result of the funding of the Competition Authority? The total amount of the supervisory fee is forecast to be EUR 1,6 million next year. That would be the financing of the economic regulator's activities. The other party to the competition authority's activities, i.e. competition supervision, remains financed from the state budget, amounting to EUR 1,1 million. Overall, the state budget funding would decrease by EUR 900000. At the same time, the funding of the Competition Authority would increase by EUR 700000: more EUR 200000 for competition monitoring and EUR 500000 for the operation of the economic regulator. This change will also allow the Competition Authority to successfully carry out new tasks, to improve the quality of its activities and to increase the efficiency of its work. Of course, other efforts are being made by the Competition Authority to be successful, effective and effective. This concerns both the restructuring of support services currently in progress and the wider use of IT solutions. In the longer term, for example, it is also important to use large data to create a better analytical capacity to identify possible distortions of competition in different markets. More high-quality market analyses are essential so that the Competition Authority can focus on problem markets and locations. The analyses shall also provide good background material for other regulatory changes, including legislative changes, where appropriate. Of course, there are those who are interested in the fact that the Competition Authority is underfunded and underbuilt and unable to carry out its tasks successfully. Hidden agreements that divide the market or agree price levels, as well as monopolies that fail to manage their activities, are a burden on the economy. Monopolisticism and the situation on the oligopolistic market, with few players, are often accompanied by lower economic efficiency and less innovativeness, technological lag, higher prices for consumers and poorer goods or services. This is, of course, when market control and regulation are inadequate. In order to avoid the negative consequences listed above for society as a whole and for consumers, the Competition Authority must be strong, supported by adequate funding, people and competences. Changing the funding of the Competition Authority is an important component that makes this agency stronger and thus supports the improvement of the competitive situation in Estonia. I therefore expect the Riigikogu to support this bill. Thank you. I'm ready to answer your questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U639	Thank you. Undoubtedly, the aim of the Competition Authority to be as small as possible can be to do as little work as possible. But in this case, we would certainly get much higher prices, and not only on a regulated market, but above all on a unregulated market with monopolistic agreements. Price regulation is known to be based on laws that specify precisely which components must be monitored. As a general rule, prices are subject to a maximum threshold above which no increase may be made. This is what the Competition Authority evaluates, analyses, looks at these numbers. Perhaps as an example, which is probably forgotten by very many, the dispute over Tallinn Water, which ended a few years ago, fits. At the time, the price was set for Tallinn Vee, the regulation was a little different, the municipal level went. When it went to the Competition Authority, he did an analysis and found that these prices were high. As a result, as a result of litigation, prices fell in Tallinn Water. And this concerned the other regions of Tallinn as well as the surrounding area. Water prices fell pretty good. Competition supervision or price control does not always mean that prices should fall. Because if the input components increase, there is justification for increasing prices. However, the role of the Competition Authority in this pricing exercise is to ensure that the monopoly does not unduly increase prices and above the level that is allowed. There must always be control on the monopoly market. If you leave the player simply to act, you can reasonably be sure that the majority of them sooner or later will, in fact, all sooner or later, start turning things in their favour. So if you really want to keep home expenses under control, you should be interested in the Competition Authority being strong so that it can be a serious opponent of the monopolies and, if necessary, a serious partner so that the price increase does not come or, if it does happen, the price increase will be limited and not taken out of the ceiling, but entirely legitimate. It's essential. We are also putting in place the need to control non-regulated markets. The Competition Authority has acted, but this is where I believe that the activities of the Competition Authority should be directed much more in the near future. These complaints and reproaches that we read in the press, perhaps we often think about it ourselves, and you yourself have, from time to time, expressed something in one area or another. It needs analysis, it needs evaluation. Whether these agreements exist and if they exist, they must be dealt with, punished as necessary. And if there aren't, you have to prove that they don't exist. The market must be examined, analysed and, if necessary, put in order.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U677	Let's move on to the agenda. Our 17th item on the agenda today is the first reading of the draft law amending state fees, civil procedure and other laws, initiated by the Government of the Republic. The rapporteur is Maris Lauri, Minister of Justice. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U678	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I will introduce to you the draft 443 amending the State Tax Act, the Code of Civil Procedure and other laws, namely the Code of Administrative Procedure and the Act on the Implementation of the Code of Civil Procedure and the Code of Enforcement Procedure. This has four major objectives: to increase state fees, to replace bail with state fees, to change the service of procedural documents and to increase the limits of the simplified procedure and of the urgent procedure for ordering payment. First, raising state fees. The draft concerns state fees relating to judicial activities, but not all of them. State fees and some others related to work and family matters have been discarded. When we talk about state fees, state fees should, by their very nature, cover the costs of the activities carried out by the State. And as costs increase, state fees should also increase. However, the State does not have to commit the amount of State fees absolutely and rigidly to the costs of the operation. It is also justified to cover part of the costs by reference to a number of other significant considerations. For example, access to essential but costly services can be mentioned. Access to justice provided for in § 15 of the Constitution is not absolute. The legislator has the right to define limits to the right of appeal within the framework laid down by the Constitution, including with regard to other constitutional values. One of the values of the constitutional order to be taken into account is procedural economics. The obligation to pay state fees for judicial proceedings has been imposed in order to avoid a large number of excessive and malicious complaints and to cover procedural costs to a reasonable extent from the amounts received by the state fees in the state budget. The aim is also to save taxpayers' money. The current court fees and registration fees were amended [2014]. ] in connection with the changeover to the euro. A large part of the current fees date back to 2006, as in 2014 the conversion of kroons into euro took place in principle and no substantive analysis or change took place. It is clear that the substantive value of these fees has changed considerably in the last 15 years. It is therefore also justified to increase the fees in order to ensure procedural economics, to avoid overburdening the courts with unjustified and malicious requirements and to better align the rates of state fees for judicial proceedings with the principle of costorientation. The bill raises state fees to a very different extent. The main increase is 40%, which corresponds to inflation. Increases of a higher percentage come on small fees, which are generally linked to registration operations. For example, the state fee will rise from EUR 4 to EUR 10 if natural persons, members or private limited liability companies change their data or supplement them in a commercial register. However, there are also minor increases, for example, the state fee for registration of a building cooperative will rise from 20 to 25 euros, i.e. 25%, and the state fee for registration of a foundation from 58 to 70 euros, i.e. 21%. In the case of state fees, it has been tried to reach rates which would be either five or zero. And once again, the state fees for family law claims, proceedings without action remain unchanged, as these procedures affect people's daily lives more, and recourse to the courts is a real need to protect vital interests and the rights enshrined in the Constitution. The second issue that the draft deals with is the replacement of bail by state fees. The term Cassation Card is used in the Code of Civil and Judicial Procedure since 1992 and 1993, when the Code of Administrative Offences of the Estonian SSR and the Code of Civil Procedure were abolished. Unfortunately, the explanatory notes do not explain why the Cassation Trade and any bail were created and why they should be distinguished from state fees. The Supreme Court has stated that the cassation concession is a deposit which should prevent the lodging of unfounded complaints. At the same time, the state fee also has at least one objective exactly the same. Judicial actions are carried out at all levels of the court and the parties seeking to act should pay for them in a uniform manner. So it has been rather a political question as to whether certain procedural procedures are subject to state fees or bail by the parties to the proceedings. The purpose of the amendment is to harmonise the costs paid by the parties at all stages of the proceedings. In civil and administrative court proceedings, it is not justified to distinguish between state fees, cassation bonds and bail as a court charge. Following the amendment, these bonds will be replaced by a state fee. Bail is also on appeal to the Supreme Court. However, due to the size of the bail, the application to the Supreme Court has become cheaper than the court of first or second instance, while the process of cassation is actually considerably more expensive than the court proceedings of first and second instance. This is because the court proceedings are conducted in the Supreme Court by a formation of at least three members, composed of the highest-paid judges. The third area of amendments concerns service of procedural documents. The delivery of procedural documents is resource-intensive as it is accompanied by substantial technical work and, in addition, the cost of postal services has increased considerably in recent years. For example, while in 2018 the postal costs of the courts of first and second instance were EUR 587000, last year EUR 1.6 million, in the first half of this year more than 930000, i.e. this year's cost could rise to 2 million. So, obviously, it is already necessary to take steps to reduce these postal costs. The amendments to the draft act will facilitate the delivery of documents to companies. According to the Commercial Code, it is the obligation to enter on the registration card the electronic address of the undertaking or company, which must also be updated if necessary and at least confirmed when submitting the annual report. It is reasonable to assume that the pre-requisite is created by the Supreme Court, that companies and companies check the e-mail addresses they receive to us every working day. However, the bill considers sending procedural documents to be an e-mail within five working days, based on the fact that smaller companies may have different work practices. The movement of documents shall also be accelerated in the urgent procedure for the order for payment and the rules relating to the service by post of procedural documents shall be adjusted. The fourth area concerns the increase of the limits of the simplified procedure and of the urgent payment order procedure. The greatest burden lies with the courts of first instance dealing with civil matters, which already provided for simplified and written procedures in 2005. A simplified procedure can be applied to dealing with small cases at the cost of an action and merely with the requirements arising from documents. Here, too, it makes sense to raise the thresholds due to changes in economic circumstances, in particular the rise in income and living standards. These limits will be raised, for example, from the current EUR 6400 to EUR 8000 in the urgent payment order procedure. However, some of these changes also concern other thresholds for the simplified procedure. The judge will continue to be able to decide which simplification procedures or possibilities he will use and whether he will use them at all. For example, there may be a situation where a relatively modest requirement is the subject of a very complex dispute and therefore the use of simplification options is not feasible at all. Why are these changes necessary? First of all, it is certainly because the amounts set as monetary amount have in many cases fallen into the way of time. It is logical that all financial limits, state fees, subsidies and other [rates] imposed by the State will be reviewed from time to time. It has to be said that the passion for raising a number of subsidies is always very politically strong, which is why they tend to rise as a much lighter decision. On the other hand, higher cost levels for people are not expected to be raised, although this should also be done. Undoubtedly, it would be better if the state fees had been raised earlier and, perhaps, repeatedly. Then these liftings would have been smaller and less emotional. But it seems to me that we have a lack of political courage over and over again. As I pointed out, the last change in state fees took place in 2014, when [the conversion of kroons into euros], no substantive assessment was carried out. Since then, GDP has increased by 25%, average wages by more than 42%, net family income by 32%, and this is from 2014 when the state fees were not actually changed. Now that we add to these percentages this and next year's forecasts, it is clear that state fees need to be increased, and it is precisely given in very prudent rates. On the substantive side, it should also be pointed out that the workload of the courts has increased and that different types of statements have increasingly been made. Some examples. 101000 applications were submitted to the Tartu County Court Registry Department last year, 88500 in the previous year, an increase of 14% in one year. The problem is that the documents presented are increasingly incomplete and need to be reviewed, reviewed and dealt with in the course of the procedure. As a result, workload has increased and resolution times have increased on average. In 2020, 33658 civil cases were received by the county court, which is the record for seven years. The length of proceedings is increasing, and 16% of cases are already pending for more than a year. An overburdened court does not understand justice quickly enough, and this naturally gives rise to resentment. It would be natural for people and businesses to be able to resolve disputes between themselves, and if this did not work, intermediaries would be contacted and the court would be the last choice. It is certainly important to get justice, but it is in the interests of society and, in fact, of those arguing, of resolving disputes as quickly as possible, with as little money and time as possible. Therefore, in very many cases, the first place to settle disputes should not be a court. If, however, this path were chosen, it would be fairer to do so, in particular, with the substantial financial contribution of the parties concerned themselves. Discussions with judges and court officials have revealed that, unfortunately, Estonia has also experienced an increasing number of processes because of the process. In Estonia, applicants can actually apply for a fee exemption if necessary, in which case the court will consider a number of circumstances, including the prospect of a dispute. The costs of the courts of first instance and second instance have increased from 33 million in 2014 to 45,3 million euros this year, or 37%. There are a number of employees who have received significantly lower wages than the labour market average: judicial interpreters, referees and others. This issue will also be addressed by the state budget for the coming year, where there will be significantly more money for these groups of employees than in the past. We are working on finding a solution and increasing the number of judges if necessary. However, the changes in the fees and the above changes are also undoubtedly essential and the courts are looking forward to them. We also use more and more IT solutions in courts, make changes to work organisation, review work processes. If necessary, we will make changes to the law. Some of these things are already happening and many things are planned. There are also several drafts in the proceedings of the Riigikogu that deal with these problems. But it has to be said that no system can be changed so that no part of it is affected at all. I therefore hope that you will support this draft. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U705	Ladies and gentlemen, The debate of the Committee on Legal Affairs on the draft law amending state fees, the Code of Civil Procedure and other laws took place on 19 October and the draft was presented by Minister of Justice Maris Lauri at the Commission. I will only highlight a few things from the Minister's presentation, which, indeed, sounded here today, but they deserve to be repeated. One is that, by way of exception, it is intended not to raise the state fees for disputes concerning families of fundamental importance to the public, so that the state fees for family law claims and proceedings without an action remain unchanged as these procedures affect people's daily lives more. And another is the fact that most of the state fees were last adjusted in 2011 when the changeover to the euro took place and, at the same time, they were actually introduced already in 2006, i.e. practically 15 years ago. During this time, the costs of the courts have increased, even because of inflation, but not just because of inflation. The Minister pointed out these increases in detail. Eneli Kivi, adviser to the Ministry's Judicial Service, who attended the meeting of the Commission, explained to the Commission that in addition to the cassation concession, all other bonds would be replaced by a state fee in the Code of Civil and Administrative Procedure. The members of the Commission wanted concrete examples where, in percentage terms, the rise in state fees is high, but relatively modest in absolute terms. The Minister said that, for example, the state fee paid to the administrative court for an appeal would increase by 33%, but it would amount to EUR 5. The state fee for the registration of a self-employed company in the commercial register will rise by 54%, but it will increase by 7 euros. The Commission has examined the fact that many people receive letters from banks and post offices, where, for example, depending on the company, it is reported that the amount of the fine will increase by EUR 5 to 10 when the letter is received. The point of the question was that it wasn't illegal to charge companies to send a letter. The Minister assured the person asking that this would facilitate the delivery of documents to the companies. No separate payment is regulated by this bill. Service of judicial documents is now and will continue to take place within the framework of this draft from the Court's budget and private individuals will not be charged this fee. The Minister also stated that this draft does not deal with the way private companies deliver documents to private individuals, but only with the way courts deliver communications to private individuals and companies. It's clear. In addition, it was examined whether the purpose of raising state fees could not be interpreted by the state as if less legal assistance was being provided. Here it is worth repeating the Minister's explanation in the Commission that the increase in state fees is linked to the fact that they have to cover operating costs in the main. The costs of courts have increased over the years. Raising state fees should help to mitigate the impact of the increase in expenditure. At the same time, people will still be able to go to court and receive legal aid from the state, which will not change. In addition, there is free legal advice, which is compensated by the State. But the most important thing is that people do not go to court with their legal problems, but resolve them earlier. That's what the Minister said. There was also a clarification of the debate on one of the contradictions highlighted by a Member of the Commission. I quote: "The state fees will also be raised in order for fewer people to go to court, but they will not be raised in the case of disputes concerning family matters, where they may also be brought to court for other motives. " The Minister explained that there are a lot of material disputes in civil disputes, where at first sight there are minor reasons for arguing. And in family disputes where, for example, custody is decided, there will be no change. In response to the question put by the Member of the Commission, it was also repeated that the delivery of documents on paper to private individuals would remain. Commission advisor Linnar Liivamägi also reminded the members of the Commission of the meaning of the state fee. Indeed, the Constitution provides everyone with a fundamental right to apply to the courts if a person considers that his or her rights or freedoms have been violated. The state fee is by its nature a restriction on this fundamental right which is justified by only one other constitutional value, namely procedural economics. In the case of the draft in question, this restriction in the form of an increase in the rates of state fees is justified and it is necessary that the court is not referred to too lightly. The chairman of the committee, Marek Jürgenson, proposed that procedural decisions be taken on the draft. It was decided to put this draft on the plenary agenda by 27 October, to propose that the first reading be closed, to propose that the deadline for amendments be set at 4 p.m. on 5 November and to appoint Urve Tiidus, Member of the Committee on Legal Affairs, as the representative of the lead committee. All these decisions were made in consensus by Marek Jürgenson, Sulev Kannimäe, Uno Kaskpeit, Tarmo Kruusimäe, Urve Tiidus and Vilja Toomast. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U709	Ladies and gentlemen, I want to make a serious criticism of the draft that has now been put on our table. In fact, the fact that, from the moment our constitution was adopted, there is a very important point to be noticed: every person has the right to appeal to the courts to defend his or her violated rights. And, of course, this right has also been used quite diligently, given the long time since the restoration of the Republic of Estonia. And I can also say that the courts have made fair decisions. These state fees were very high in financial terms and were usually supplemented by legal aid services provided by a lawyer who had to be paid several times as much as possible. That means it's pretty expensive to go to court. At one point, it was realised that we were violating the meaning of the Constitution and preventing persons with high state fees from going to court. And then at one point, the state fees were reduced. And the result was that the meaning of the Constitution was fulfilled. Visiting the court also made it more possible for those who did not have a wallet on a tree. Which one of us hasn't heard the sentence that I'm not rich enough to go to court. The first is that an increase in the rates of court fees is necessary in order to update the state fees relating to judicial proceedings in such a way that the applicable rates of state fees partially cover the Court's operating costs. Here we have reached a situation where people are trying to fill public budget holes with the help of those who have turned to the court, with the help of people's sense of justice and justice. This is not appropriate for the Constitution. The second whale is to rule out malicious or manifestly perverted appeals to the courts. Based on my practice, which has been 24 years, I can say: no one, no one, will go to court maliciously. It spends a lot of nerves, plus, of course, financial resources. It's really better not to go there for health. I can certainly say on this point that this statement is absurd. Then it's a question of a lack of perspective in court. Prospective or not? Of course, there are always two parties in court: one is the plaintiff, the other is the defendant in civil proceedings and each is certain of its own law. We never know whether it's prospective or not. It is often the case that unimaginable things turn out to be a winner in a court battle. It all depends on how your rights are exercised. ParlaMint-EE_2021-10-27_U714	Thank you, Mr President-in-Office. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! I am pleased to introduce to you the purpose of the building code and, in connection with it, the draft law amending other laws. The aim is to clarify the rules on the processing of building permits for the loading of a public body of water, to supplement the existing regulation on the acceptance and refusal of a building permit, as well as on the withdrawal of a building permit. The processing of competing applications will be improved and clarified. The amendments are intended to complement the law with regulation, which allows the State itself to carry out preparatory activities for the construction and building permits of the sea for the development of a sea wind park and aquaculture and to submit their building permits to a public auction or a selection offer. Existing regulations are also specified and supplemented in the view of ensuring economic security, national defence restrictions and monitoring conditions. The draft amends the Building Code, the Penal Code, the Electricity Market Act, the Water Act, the Land Valuation Act and the Implementation Act of the Construction Code and the Planning Act. Honourable members of the Riigikogu, I ask you to support the draft.
